 
 [2312.05658] Monoid Theory in Alonzo
A Little Theories Formalization in Simple Type Theory 
 Monoid Theory in Alonzo
 A Little Theories Formalization in Simple Type Theory 
 William M. Farmer 
 Thanks:  Address: Department of Computing and Software, McMaster
University, 1280 Main Street West, Hamilton, Ontario L8S 4L7,
Canada. Email: wmfarmer@mcmaster.ca ,

 Dennis Y. Zvigelsky 1 1 footnotemark: 
 1 
 November 13, 2024
 Abstract 
 Alonzo is a practice-oriented classical higher-order logic that
extends first-order logic and that admits undefined expressions.
Named in honor of Alonzo Church, Alonzo is based on Church’s type
theory, Church’s formulation of simple type theory. The little
theories method is a method for formalizing mathematical knowledge as
a theory graph consisting of theories as nodes and
 theory morphisms as directed edges. The development of a
mathematical topic is done in the “little theory” in the theory
graph that has the most convenient level of abstraction and the most
convenient vocabulary, and then the definitions and theorems produced
in the development are transported, as needed, to other theories via
the theory morphisms in the theory graph. The purpose of this paper
is to illustrate how a body of mathematical knowledge can be
formalized in Alonzo using the little theories method. This is done
by formalizing monoid theory — the body of mathematical
knowledge about monoids — in Alonzo.
 Keywords: formal mathematics, simple type theory, little
theories method, monoids, theory graphs, mathematical knowledge
management.
 MSC classification codes: 03B16, 03B38, 68V20, 68V30.

 1 Introduction
 Formal mathematics is mathematics done within a formal logic.
 Formalization is the act of expressing mathematical knowledge
in a formal logic. One of the chief benefits of formal mathematics is
that a body of mathematical knowledge can be formalized as a precise,
rigorous, and highly organized structure. This structure records the
relationships between mathematical ideas and the contexts that
govern mathematical results. Since it is based on a formal logic,
it can be developed and analyzed using software.
 An attractive and powerful method for organizing mathematical
knowledge is the little theories method   [ 21 ] .
A body of mathematical knowledge is represented in the form of a
 theory graph   [ 31 ] consisting of
 theories as nodes and theory morphisms as directed
edges. Each mathematical topic is developed in the “little theory”
in the theory graph that has the most convenient level of abstraction
and the most convenient vocabulary. Then the definitions and theorems
produced in the development are transported, as needed, from this
abstract theory to other, usually more concrete, theories in the graph
via the theory morphisms in the graph.
 The purpose of this paper is to illustrate how a body of mathematical
knowledge can be formalized in Alonzo  [ 20 ] , a
practice-oriented classical higher-order logic that extends
first-order logic, using the little theories method. Named in honor
of Alonzo Church, Alonzo is based on Church’s type
theory  [ 8 ] , Church’s formulation of simple type
theory  [ 17 ] , and is closely related to Peter Andrews’
 𝒬 0 {\cal Q}_{0}   [ 1 ] and
LUTINS  [ 13 , 14 , 15 ] , the logic of the IMPS
proof assistant  [ 22 ] . Unlike traditional predicate
logics, Alonzo admits partial functions and undefined expressions in
accordance with the approach employed in mathematical practice that we
call the traditional approach to undefinedness   [ 16 ] .
Since partial functions naturally arise from theory
morphisms  [ 15 ] , the little theories method works best with
a logic like Alonzo that supports partial functions.
 Alonzo has a simple syntax with a formal notation for machines
and a compact notation for humans that closely resembles the
notation found in mathematical practice. The compact notation is
defined by the extensive set of notational definitions and
conventions given in  [ 20 ] . Alonzo has two semantics, one
for mathematics based on standard models and one for logic
based on Henkin-style general models   [ 28 ] . By
virtue of its syntax and semantics, Alonzo is exceptionally well
suited for expressing and reasoning about mathematical ideas and, in
particular, mathematical structures.
 We have chosen monoid theory — the concepts, properties, and
facts about monoids — as a sample body of mathematical knowledge to
formalize in Alonzo. A monoid is a mathematical structure
consisting of a nonempty set, an associative binary function on the
set, and a member of the set that is an identity element with respect
to the function. Monoids are abundant in mathematics and computing.
Single-object categories are monoids. Groups are monoids in which
every element has an inverse. And several algebraic structures, such
as rings, fields, Boolean algebras, and vector spaces, contain monoids
as substructures.
 Since a monoid is a significantly simpler algebraic structure than a
group, monoid theory lacks the rich structure of group theory. We are
formalizing monoid theory in Alonzo, instead of group theory, since it
has just enough structure to adequately illustrate how a body of
mathematical knowledge can be formalized in Alonzo. We will see that
employing the little theories method in the formalization of monoid
theory in Alonzo naturally leads to a robust theory graph.
 Alonzo is equipped with a set of mathematical knowledge modules 
( modules for short) for constructing various kinds of
mathematical knowledge units. For example, it has modules for
constructing “theories” and “theory morphisms”. A language 
(or signature ) of Alonzo is a pair L = ( ℬ , 𝒞 ) L=({\cal B},{\cal C}) , where ℬ {\cal B} 
is a finite set of base types and 𝒞 {\cal C} is a set of constants, that
specifies a set of expressions. A theory of Alonzo is a pair
 T = ( L , Γ ) T=(L,\Gamma) where L L is a language called the language of
 T T and Γ \Gamma is a set of sentences of L L called the axioms
of T T . And a theory morphism from a theory T 1 T_{1} to a theory
 T 2 T_{2} is a mapping of the expressions of T 1 T_{1} to the expressions of
 T 2 T_{2} such that (1) base types are mapped to types and closed
quasitypes (expressions that denote sets of values), (2) constants are
mapped to closed expressions of appropriate type, and (3) valid
sentences are mapped to valid sentences.
 Alonzo also has modules for constructing “developments” and
“development morphisms”. A theory development (or
 development for short) of Alonzo is a pair D = ( T , Ξ ) D=(T,\Xi) where
 T T is a theory and Ξ \Xi is a (possibly empty) sequence of
definitions and theorems presented, respectively, as definition and
theorem packages (see  [ 20 , Section 11.1] ). T T is called
the bottom theory of D D , and T ′ T^{\prime} , the extension of T T 
obtained by adding the definitions in Ξ \Xi to  T T , is called the
 top theory of D D . We say that D D is a development of
 T T . A development morphism from a development D 1 D_{1} to a
development D 2 D_{2} is a mapping from the expressions of D 1 D_{1} to the
expressions of D 2 D_{2} that restricts to a theory morphism from the
bottom theory of D 1 D_{1} to the bottom theory of D 2 D_{2} and that uniquely
extends to a theory morphism from the top theory of D 1 D_{1} to the top
theory of D 2 D_{2} (see  [ 20 , Section 13.4.1] ). Theories and
theory morphisms are special cases of developments and development
morphisms, respectively, since we identify a theory T T with the
trivial development ( T , [ ] ) (T,[\,]) .
 The modules for constructing developments and development morphisms
provide the means to represent knowledge in the form of a
 development graph , a richer kind of theory graph, in which the
nodes are developments and the directed edges are development
morphisms. Alonzo includes modules for transporting definitions and
theorems from one development to another via development morphisms.
The design of Alonzo’s module system is inspired by the IMPS
implementation of the little theories
method  [ 21 , 22 ] .
 Although  [ 20 ] presents a simple and elegant proof system
for Alonzo which is derived from Peter Andrews’ proof system for
 𝒬 0 {\cal Q}_{0}   [ 1 ] , in this paper we validate the definitions
and theorems in a development using traditional mathematical proof.
In this paper, the proofs are not included in the modules used to
construct developments. Instead, they are given separately in
Appendix  A . Alonzo has not been implemented as a
proof assistant. However, since Alonzo is closely related to
LUTINS  [ 13 , 14 , 15 ] , the logic of the IMPS proof
assistant  [ 22 ] , Alonzo can be implemented in much the
same way that LUTINS is implemented in IMPS.
 We produced the formalization of monoid theory with just a minimal
amount of software support. We used the set of LaTeX macros and
environments for Alonzo given in  [ 19 ] plus a few macros
created specifically for this paper. The macros are for presenting
Alonzo types and expressions in both the formal and compact notations.
The environments are for presenting Alonzo mathematical knowledge
modules. The Alonzo modules are printed in brown color.
 Alonzo is fully presented in  [ 20 ] . Due to space
limitations, we cannot duplicate the presentation of Alonzo in this
paper. So we assume that the reader is familiar
with  [ 20 ] . 1 1

 Table 3: Notational Definitions for Monoids: Abbreviations 
 The standard approach to formal mathematics emphasizes
 certification : (1) mathematics is done with the help of a proof
assistant and (2) all details are formally proved and mechanically
checked. The formalization of monoid theory presented in this paper
exemplifies an alternative approach to formal
mathematics  [ 18 ] that emphasizes communication :
(1) proofs are written in a traditional (nonformal) style, (2) the
underlying logic is designed to be as close to mathematical practice
as possible, (3) mathematical knowledge is organized as a development
graph using the little theories method, and (4) supporting software
systems do not need a formal proof system and thus are much easier to
build and use than proof assistants. The result is an approach in
which everything is done within a formal logic except for proofs and
the entire development is optimized for communication. We will call
the standard approach the certification-oriented approach and
the alternative approach the communication-oriented approach .
 The paper is organized as follows.
Sections  2 – 9 present
developments of theories of monoids, commutative monoids,
transformation monoids, monoid actions, monoid homomorphisms, and
monoids over real number arithmetic, respectively, plus some
supporting developments. These developments have been constructed to
be illustrative; they are not intended to be complete in any sense.
Sections  2 – 9 also present
various development morphisms that are used to transport definitions
and theorems from one development to another.
Section  10 shows how our formalization of monoid theory
can support a theory of strings. Related work is discussed in
Section  11 . The paper concludes in
Section  12 with a summary and some final remarks. The
definitions and theorems of the developments we have constructed are
validated by traditional mathematical proofs presented in
Appendix  A . Appendix  B contains
some miscellaneous theorems needed for the proofs in
Appendix  A .
 2 Monoids
 A monoid is a mathematical structure ( m , ⋅ , e ) (m,\cdot,e) where m m is
a nonempty set of values, ⋅ : ( m × m ) → m \cdot:(m\times m)\rightarrow m is an
associative function, and e ∈ m e\in m is an identity element with
respect to ⋅ \cdot . Mathematics and computing are replete with
examples of monoids such as ( ℕ , + , 0 ) (\mathbb{N},+,0) , ( ℕ , ∗ , 1 ) (\mathbb{N},*,1) , and
 ( Σ ∗ , + ⁣ + , ϵ ) (\Sigma^{*},\mathrel{++},\epsilon) where Σ ∗ \Sigma^{*} is the set of
strings over an alphabet Σ \Sigma , + ⁣ + \mathrel{++} is string
concatenation, and ϵ \epsilon is the empty string.
 Let T = ( L , Γ ) T=(L,\Gamma) be a theory 2 2 
 2 
 A theory of Alonzo
and related notions are presented in Chapter 9 of  [ 20 ] . of
Alonzo. Consider a tuple
 ( ζ α , 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α , 𝐄 α ) (\zeta_{\alpha},\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}) 
 where (1) ζ α \zeta_{\alpha} is either
a type α \alpha of L L or a closed quasitype 𝐐 { α } \mathbf{Q}_{\{\alpha\}} of L L and (2)  𝐅 ( α × α ) → α \mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}} and 𝐄 α \mathbf{E}_{\alpha} are closed
expressions of L L . Let 𝐗 o \mathbf{X}_{o} be the sentence

 where
 MONOID is the abbreviation introduced by the notational
definition given in Table  3 and
 𝐌 { α } \mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}} is U { α } U_{\{\alpha\}} if
 ζ α \zeta_{\alpha} is α \alpha and is 𝐐 { α } \mathbf{Q}_{\{\alpha\}} 
otherwise. If T ⊨ 𝐗 o T\vDash\mathbf{X}_{o} , then ( ζ α , 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α , 𝐄 α ) (\zeta_{\alpha},\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}) denotes a monoid in T T . Stated more precisely,
if T ⊨ 𝐗 o T\vDash\mathbf{X}_{o} , then, for all general models M M of T T 
and all assignments φ ∈ 𝖺𝗌𝗌𝗂𝗀𝗇 ⁡ ( M ) \varphi\in\mathsf{assign}(M) , ( ζ α , 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α , 𝐄 α ) (\zeta_{\alpha},\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}) denotes the
monoid
 ( V φ M ​ ( 𝐌 { α } ) , V φ M ​ ( 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α ) , V φ M ​ ( 𝐄 α ) ) . (V^{M}_{\varphi}(\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}}),V^{M}_{\varphi}(\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}}),V^{M}_{\varphi}(\mathbf{E}_{\alpha})). 
 Thus we can show that ( ζ α , 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α , 𝐄 α ) (\zeta_{\alpha},\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}) denotes a
monoid in T T by proving T ⊨ 𝐗 o T\vDash\mathbf{X}_{o} . However, we may
need general definitions and theorems about monoids to prove
properties in  T T about the monoid denoted by ( ζ α , 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α , 𝐄 α ) (\zeta_{\alpha},\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}) . It would be extremely inefficient to state
these definitions and prove these theorems in T T since instances of
these same definitions and theorems could easily be needed for other
triples in T T , as well as in other theories, that denote monoids.
 Instead of developing part of a monoid theory in T T , we should apply
the little theories method and develop a “little theory” T mon T_{\rm mon} of monoids, separate from  T T , that has the most convenient
level of abstraction and the most convenient vocabulary for talking
about monoids. The general definitions and theorems of monoids can
then be introduced in a development 3 3 
 3 
 A development of
Alonzo and related notions are presented in Chapter 11
of  [ 20 ] . D mon D_{\rm mon} of T mon T_{\rm mon} in a universal
abstract form. When these definitions and theorems are needed in a
development D D , a development morphism 4 4 
 4 
 A theory
morphism and a development morphism of Alonzo are presented in
Sections 13.3 and 13.4, respectively, of  [ 20 ] . from
 D mon D_{\rm mon} to D D can be created and then used to transport the
abstract definitions and theorems in D mon D_{\rm mon} to concrete
instances of them in D D . The validity of these concrete definitions
and theorems in D D is guaranteed by the fact that the abstract
definitions and theorems are valid in the top theory of D mon D_{\rm mon} 
and the development morphism used to transport them preserves
validity.
 We can verify that ( ζ α , 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α , 𝐄 α ) (\zeta_{\alpha},\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}) denotes a
monoid in T T by simply constructing an appropriate theory morphism
 Φ \Phi from T mon T_{\rm mon} to T T . As a bonus, we can use Φ \Phi to
transport the abstract definitions and theorems in T mon T_{\rm mon} to
concrete instances of them in T T (or a development of T T ) whenever
they are needed. Moreover, we do not have to explicitly prove that
 𝐗 o \mathbf{X}_{o} is valid in  T T ; instead, we only need to show that
there is an abstract theorem of T mon T_{\rm mon} that Φ \Phi transports
to 𝐗 o \mathbf{X}_{o} .
 The following theory definition module defines a suitably abstract
theory of monoids named MON :
 Theory Definition 2.1 (Monoids) 
 Name: MON . 
 Base types: M M . 
 Constants: ⋅ ( M × M ) → M , 𝖾 M \cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}},\;\mathsf{e}_{M} . 
 Axioms: 
 1. 
 ∀ x , y , z : M . x ⋅ ( y ⋅ z ) = ( x ⋅ y ) ⋅ z {\forall\,x,y,z:M\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{({y}\mathrel{\cdot}{z})}}={{({x}\mathrel{\cdot}{y})}\mathrel{\cdot}{z}}} 
 ( ⋅ \cdot is associative). 
 2. 
 ∀ x : M . 𝖾 ⋅ x = x ⋅ 𝖾 = x {\forall\,x:M\mathrel{.}{{\mathsf{e}}\mathrel{\cdot}{x}}\mathrel{=}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{\mathsf{e}}}\mathrel{=}{x}} ( 𝖾 \mathsf{e} is an identity element with respect to ⋅ \cdot ). 
 Notice that we have employed several notational definitions and
conventions in the axioms — including dropping the types of the
constants — for the sake of brevity. This theory specifies the set
of monoids exactly: The base type M M , like all types, denotes a
nonempty set m m ; the constant ⋅ ( M × M ) → M \cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}} denotes a function ⋅ : ( m × m ) → m \cdot:(m\times m)\rightarrow m that is
associative; and the constant 𝖾 m \mathsf{e}_{m} denotes a member e e of
 m m that is an identity element with respect to ⋅ \cdot .

named MON-1 , of the theory MON :

 x

parametric pseudoconstant set-op ( ( α × β ) → γ ) → ( ( { α } × { β } ) → { γ } ) {{\textsf{set-op}}_{{({({\alpha}\times{\beta})}\rightarrow{\gamma})}\rightarrow{({({\{\alpha\}}\times{\{\beta\}})}\rightarrow{\{\gamma\}})}}} defined in Table  2 .
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟣 \mathsf{Thm1} states that each model of MON defines a
monoid. 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟤 \mathsf{Thm2} states the monoid’s binary function is total.
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟥 \mathsf{Thm3} states that a monoid’s identity element is
unique. 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟣 \mathsf{Def1} defines the notion of a submonoid and
 Thm4–Thm6 are three theorems about submonoids. Notice that
 ⋅ ↾ s × s \cdot\mbox{$\upharpoonright$}\nolinebreak_{{s}\times{s}} , the restriction of ⋅ \cdot to
 s × s {s}\times{s} , denotes a partial function. Notice also that
 ⋅ ↾ s × s ↓ ( s × s ) → s {{\cdot\mbox{$\upharpoonright$}_{{s}\times{s}}}\downarrow{{({s}\times{s})}\rightarrow{s}}} 
 in 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟣 \mathsf{Def1} asserts that
 s s is closed under ⋅ ↾ s × s {\cdot\mbox{$\upharpoonright$}_{{s}\times{s}}} since
 ⋅ \cdot is total by 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟤 \mathsf{Thm2} . 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟤 \mathsf{Def2} 
defines  ⋅ op ( M × M ) → M \cdot^{\rm op}_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}} , the
 opposite of ⋅ \cdot , and Thm7–Thm8 are key theorems
about  ⋅ op \cdot^{\rm op} . 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟥 \mathsf{Def3} defines  ⊙ ( { M } × { M } ) → { M } \odot_{{({\{M\}}\times{\{M\}})}\rightarrow{\{M\}}} , the
 set product on { M } {\{M\}} ; 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟦 \mathsf{Def4} 
defines  𝖤 { M } \mathsf{E}_{\{M\}} , the identity element with respect
to ⊙ \odot ; and Thm9–Thm10 are key theorems about ⊙ \odot .
These four definitions and ten theorems require proofs that show the
RHS of each definition (i.e., the definition’s definiens) is defined
and each theorem is valid. The proofs are given in
Appendix  A .
 3 Transportation of Definitions and Theorems
 Let T T be a theory such that T ⊨ 𝐗 o T\vDash\mathbf{X}_{o} where
 𝐗 o \mathbf{X}_{o} is
 𝖬𝖮𝖭𝖮𝖨𝖣 ⁡ ( 𝐌 { α } , 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α , 𝐄 α ) , {\mathsf{MONOID}(\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}},\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha})}, 
 and assume that D D is some development of
 T T (which could be T T itself). We would like to show how the
definitions and theorems of the development MON-1 can be
transported to D D . 5 5 
 5 
 A transportation is presented in
Subsection 13.4.2 of  [ 20 ] . 
 Before considering the general case, we will consider the special case
when 𝐌 { α } \mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}} is U { α } U_{\{\alpha\}} , which
denotes the entire domain for the type α \alpha , and
 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α \mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}} and
 𝐄 α \mathbf{E}_{\alpha} are constants 𝐜 ( α × α ) → α \mathbf{c}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}} and  𝐝 α \mathbf{d}_{\alpha} . We start
by defining a theory morphism from MON to T T using a theory
translation definition module:
 Theory Translation Definition 3.1 (Special MON to T T ) 
 Name: special-MON-to- T T . 
 Source theory: MON . 
 Target theory: T T . 
 Base type mapping: 
 1. 
 M ↦ α M\mapsto\alpha . 
 Constant mapping: 
 1. 
 ⋅ ( M × M ) → M ↦ 𝐜 ( α × α ) → α {\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}}\mapsto{\mathbf{c}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}}} . 
 2. 
 𝖾 M ↦ 𝐝 α {\mathsf{e}_{M}}\mapsto{\mathbf{d}_{\alpha}} . 
 Since special-MON-to- T T is a normal translation 6 6 
 6 
 A
 theory translation and a development translation of
Alonzo are presented in Subsections 13.3.1 and 13.4.1, respectively,
of  [ 20 ] . , it has no obligations of the first kind
by  [ 20 , Lemma 13.10] and two obligations of the second kind
which are valid in T T by  [ 20 , Lemma 13.11] . It has two
obligations of the third kind corresponding to the two axioms of
 MON . T ⊨ 𝐗 o T\vDash\mathbf{X}_{o} implies that each of these
two obligations is valid in T T . Therefore,
 special-MON-to- T T is a theory morphism from MON to
 T T by the Morphism Theorem [ 20 , Theorem
13.13] . 7 7 
 7 
 An obligation of a theory
translation and the Morphism Theorem are presented in Subsection
13.3.2 of  [ 20 ] .

 We will next consider the general case when 𝐌 { α } \mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}} may be different from U { α } U_{\{\alpha\}} and
 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α \mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}} and
 𝐄 α \mathbf{E}_{\alpha} may not be constants. The general case is
usually more complicated and less succinct than the special case.
We start again by defining a theory morphism from MON to T T 
using a theory translation definition module:
 Theory Translation Definition 3.4 (General MON to T T ) 
 Name: general-MON-to- T T . 
 Source theory: MON . 
 Target theory: T T . 
 Base type mapping: 
 1. 
 M ↦ 𝐌 { α } M\mapsto{\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}}} . 
 Constant mapping: 
 1. 
 ⋅ ( M × M ) → M ↦ 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α {\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}}\mapsto{\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}}} . 
 2. 
 𝖾 M ↦ 𝐄 α {\mathsf{e}_{M}}\mapsto{\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}} . 
 Let general-MON-to- T = ( μ , ν ) \textsf{general-MON-to-$T$}=(\mu,\nu) . Then
 general-MON-to- T T has the following five obligations (one of
the first, two of the second, and two of the third kind):
 1. 
 ν ¯ ( U { M } ≠ ∅ { M } ) ≡ ( λ x : 𝐌 { α } . T o ) ≠ ( λ x : 𝐌 { α } . F o ) \overline{\nu}({U_{\{M\}}}\not={\emptyset_{\{M\}}})\equiv{{(\lambda\,x:\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}}\mathrel{.}T_{o})}\not={(\lambda\,x:\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}}\mathrel{.}F_{o})}} .
 2. 
 ν ¯ ( ⋅ ( M × M ) → M ↓ U { ( M × M ) → M } ) ≡ \overline{\nu}({\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}}\downarrow{U_{\{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}\}}})\equiv{} 
   𝐅 ( α × α ) → α ↓ ( λ x : ( 𝐌 { α } × 𝐌 { α } ) → 𝐌 { α } . T o ) {{\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}}}\downarrow{(\lambda\,x:{({\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}}}\times{\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}}})}\rightarrow{\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}}}\mathrel{.}T_{o})}} .
 3. 
 ν ¯ ( 𝖾 M ↓ U { M } ) ≡ 𝐄 α ↓ ( λ x : 𝐌 { α } . T o ) \overline{\nu}({\mathsf{e}_{M}}\downarrow{U_{\{M\}}})\equiv{{\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}}\downarrow{(\lambda\,x:\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}}\mathrel{.}T_{o})}} .
 4. 
 ν ¯ ( ∀ x , y , z : M . x ⋅ ( y ⋅ z ) = ( x ⋅ y ) ⋅ z ) ≡ \overline{\nu}(\forall\,x,y,z:M\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{({y}\mathrel{\cdot}{z})}}={{({x}\mathrel{\cdot}{y})}\mathrel{\cdot}{z}})\equiv{} 
 ∀ x , y , z : 𝐌 { α } . {\forall\,x,y,z:\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}}\mathrel{.}} 
 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α ​ ( x , 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α ​ ( y , z ) ) = 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α ​ ( 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α ​ ( x , y ) , z ) {{\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}}\,(x,\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}}\,(y,z))}={\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}}\,(\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}}\,(x,y),z)}} .
 5. 
 ν ¯ ( ∀ x : M . 𝖾 ⋅ x = x ⋅ 𝖾 = x ) ≡ \overline{\nu}(\forall\,x:M\mathrel{.}{{\mathsf{e}}\mathrel{\cdot}{x}}\mathrel{=}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{\mathsf{e}}}\mathrel{=}{x})\equiv{} 
 ∀ x : 𝐌 { α } . 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α ​ ( 𝐄 α , x ) = 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α ​ ( x , 𝐄 α ) = x {\forall\,x:\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}}\mathrel{.}{\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}}\,(\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},x)}\mathrel{=}{\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}}\,(x,\mathbf{E}_{\alpha})}\mathrel{=}{x}} .
 𝐀 α ≡ 𝐁 α \mathbf{A}_{\alpha}\equiv\mathbf{B}_{\alpha} means the expressions
denoted by 𝐀 α \mathbf{A}_{\alpha} and 𝐁 α \mathbf{B}_{\alpha} are identical.
 T ⊨ 𝐗 o T\vDash\mathbf{X}_{o} implies that each of these obligations is
valid in T T as follows. The first and second conjuncts of
 𝐗 o \mathbf{X}_{o} imply that the first obligation is valid in T T by
part 3 of Lemma  B.2 . The first and third conjuncts
imply that the second obligation is valid in T T by part 5 of
Lemma  B.2 . The first and fourth conjuncts imply
that the third obligation is valid in T T by part 5 of
Lemma  B.2 . And the fifth and sixth conjuncts imply,
respectively, that the fourth and fifth obligations are valid in  T T .
Therefore, general-MON-to- T T is a theory morphism by the
Morphism Theorem [ 20 , Theorem 13.13] .
 We can now transport, as before, the definitions and theorems of
 MON-1 to D D via general-MON-to- T T using
definition and theorem transportation modules, but we can also
transport them using a group transportation module 9 9 
 9

of module, which is not given in  [ 20 ] , transports a set of
definitions and theorems in parallel. . For example, 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟥 \mathsf{Thm3} 
and 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟣 \mathsf{Def1} can be transported as a group using the following
group transportation module:
 Group Transportation 3.5 (Transport of Thm3 and Def1 to D D ) 
 Name: uniqueness-of-identity-element-and-submonoid-to- D D . 
 Source development: MON-1 . 
 Target development: D D . 
 Development morphism: general-MON-to- T T .

 The abbreviation 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝐌 { α } ) {{\cal P}(\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}})} , which

 4 Opposite and Set Monoids
 For every monoid ( m , ⋅ , e ) (m,\cdot,e) , there is (1) an associated monoid
 ( m , ⋅ op , e ) (m,\cdot^{\rm op},e) , where ⋅ op \cdot^{\rm op} is the opposite of
 ⋅ \cdot , called the opposite monoid of ( m , ⋅ , e ) (m,\cdot,e) and (2) a
monoid ( 𝒫 ⁡ ( m ) , ⊙ , { e } ) ({\cal P}(m),\odot,\{e\}) , where 𝒫 ⁡ ( m ) {\cal P}(m) is the power set of
 m m and ⊙ \odot is the set product on 𝒫 ⁡ ( m ) {\cal P}(m) , called the set
monoid of ( m , ⋅ , e ) (m,\cdot,e) .
 We will construct a development morphism named
 MON-to-opposite-monoid from the theory MON to its
development MON-1 that maps
 ( M , ⋅ ( M × M ) → M , 𝖾 ) (M,\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}},\mathsf{e}) 
 to
 ( M , ⋅ ( M × M ) → M op , 𝖾 ) . (M,{\cdot^{\rm op}_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}},{\mathsf{e}}). 
 Then we will be able to
use this morphism to transport abstract definitions and theorems about
monoids to more concrete definitions and theorems about opposite
monoids. Here is the definition of MON-to-opposite-monoid :
 Development Translation Definition 4.1 ( MON to Op. Monoid) 
 Name: MON-to-opposite-monoid . 
 Source development: MON . 
 Target development: MON-1 . 
 Base type mapping: 
 1. 
 M ↦ M M\mapsto M . 
 Constant mapping: 
 1. 
 ⋅ ( M × M ) → M ↦ ⋅ op ( M × M ) → M {\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}}\mapsto{\cdot^{\rm op}_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}} . 
 2. 
 𝖾 M ↦ 𝖾 M {\mathsf{e}_{M}}\mapsto{\mathsf{e}_{M}} . 
 Since MON-to-opposite-monoid is a normal translation, it has
no obligations of the first kind by  [ 20 , Lemma 13.10] and
two obligations of the second kind which are valid in the top theory
of MON-1 by  [ 20 , Lemma 13.11] . It has two
obligations of the third kind corresponding to the two axioms of
 MON . These two obligations are logically equivalent to
 Thm7 and Thm8 , respectively, in
 MON-1 , and so these two theorems are obviously valid
in the top theory of MON-1 . Therefore,
 MON-to-opposite-monoid is a development morphism from
 MON to MON-1 by the Morphism Theorem
 [ 20 , Theorem 13.13] .
 We can now transport Thm1 via MON-to-opposite-monoid 
to show that opposite monoids are indeed monoids:
 Theorem Transportation 4.2 (Transport of Thm1 to MON-1 ) 
 Name: monoid-via-MON-to-opposite-monoid . 
 Source development: MON . 
 Target development: MON-1 . 
 Development morphism: MON-to-opposite-monoid . 
 Theorem: 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟣 \mathsf{Thm1} : 𝖬𝖮𝖭𝖮𝖨𝖣 ( U { M } , ⋅ ( M × M ) → M , 𝖾 M ) {\mathsf{MONOID}(U_{\{M\}},\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}},\mathsf{e}_{M})} 
 x (models of MON define monoids). 
 Transported theorem: 
 Thm11 (Thm1-via-MON-to-opposite-monoid) : 
    𝖬𝖮𝖭𝖮𝖨𝖣 ( U { M } , ⋅ ( M × M ) → M op , 𝖾 M ) {\mathsf{MONOID}(U_{\{M\}},\cdot^{\rm op}_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}},\mathsf{e}_{M})} 
 (opposite monoids are monoids). 
 New target development: MON-2 . 
 Similarly, we will construct a development morphism named
 MON-to-set-monoid from the theory MON to its
development MON-2 that maps
 ( M , ⋅ ( M × M ) → M , 𝖾 M ) (M,{\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}},{\mathsf{e}_{M}}) 
 to
 ( { M } , ⊙ ( { M } × { M } ) → { M } , 𝖤 { M } ) . ({\{M\}},{\odot_{{({\{M\}}\times{\{M\}})}\rightarrow{\{M\}}}},{\mathsf{E}_{\{M\}}}). 
 Then we will be able to
use this morphism to transport abstract definitions and theorems about
monoids to more concrete definitions and theorems about set monoids.
Here is the definition of MON-to-set-monoid :
 Development Translation Definition 4.3 ( MON to Set Monoid) 
 Name: MON-to-set-monoid . 
 Source development: MON . 
 Target development: MON-2 . 
 Base type mapping: 
 1. 
 M ↦ { M } M\mapsto{\{M\}} . 
 Constant mapping: 
 1. 
 ⋅ ( M × M ) → M ↦ ⊙ ( { M } × { M } ) → { M } \cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}\mapsto{\odot_{{({\{M\}}\times{\{M\}})}\rightarrow{\{M\}}}} . 
 2. 
 𝖾 M ↦ 𝖤 { M } \mathsf{e}_{M}\mapsto{\mathsf{E}_{\{M\}}} . 
 Since MON-to-set-monoid is a normal translation, it has no
obligations of the first kind by  [ 20 , Lemma 13.10] . It has
two obligations of the second kind. The first one is valid in the top
theory of MON-2 by part 4 of
Lemma  B.2 since ⊙ ( { M } × { M } ) → { M } {\odot_{{({\{M\}}\times{\{M\}})}\rightarrow{\{M\}}}} beta-reduces
by  [ 20 , Axiom A4] to a function abstraction which is
defined by  [ 20 , Axiom A5.11] . The second one is valid in
the top theory of MON-2 by part 4 of
Lemma  B.2 since 𝖤 { M } {\mathsf{E}_{\{M\}}} is a
function abstraction which is defined by  [ 20 , Axiom A5.11] .
It has two obligations of the third kind corresponding to the two
axioms of MON . These two obligations are Thm9 and
 Thm10 , respectively, in MON-2 , and so these
two theorems are obviously valid in the top theory of
 MON-2 . Therefore, MON-to-set-monoid is a
development morphism from MON to MON-2 by the
Morphism Theorem [ 20 , Theorem 13.13] .
 We can now transport Thm1 via MON-to-set-monoid to
show that set monoids are indeed monoids:
 Theorem Transportation 4.4 (Transport of Thm1 to MON-2 ) 
 Name: monoid-via-MON-to-set-monoid .

 x (models of MON define monoids). 
 Transported theorem: 
 Thm12 (Thm1-via-MON-to-set-monoid) : 
 𝖬𝖮𝖭𝖮𝖨𝖣 ( U { { M } } , ⊙ ( { M } × { M } ) → { M } , 𝖤 { M } ) {\mathsf{MONOID}(U_{\{\{M\}\}},\odot_{{({\{M\}}\times{\{M\}})}\rightarrow{\{M\}}},\mathsf{E}_{\{M\}})} 
 x (set monoids are monoids). 
 New target development: MON-3 . 
 5 Commutative Monoids
 A monoid ( m , ⋅ , e ) (m,\cdot,e) is commutative if ⋅ \cdot is
commutative.
 Let 𝐘 o \mathbf{Y}_{o} be the formula
 COM-MONOID ​ ( 𝐌 { α } , 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α , 𝐄 α ) , \textsf{COM-MONOID}(\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}},\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}), 
 where COM-MONOID is
the abbreviation introduced by the notational definition given in
Table  3 . 𝐘 o \mathbf{Y}_{o} asserts that
 ( m , ⋅ , e ) (m,\cdot,e) is a commutative monoid where m m is a set denoting
 𝐌 { α } \mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}} , ⋅ : ( m × m ) → m \cdot:(m\times m)\rightarrow m is a
function denoting 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α \mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}} , and e e is an element of m m denoting
 𝐄 α \mathbf{E}_{\alpha} .
 We can define a theory of commutative monoids, named COM-MON ,
by adding an axiom that says ⋅ \cdot is commutative to the theory
 MON using a theory extension module:
 Theory Extension 5.1 (Commutative Monoids) 
 Name: COM-MON . 
 Extends MON . 
 New base types: 
 New constants: 
 New axioms: 
 3. 
 ∀ x , y : M . x ⋅ y = y ⋅ x {\forall\,x,y:M\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{y}}={{y}\mathrel{\cdot}{x}}} ( ⋅ \cdot is commutative). 
 Then we can develop the theory COM-MON using the following
development definition module:
 Development Definition 5.2 (Commutative Monoids 1) 
 Name: COM-MON-1 . 
 Bottom theory: COM-MON . 
 Definitions and theorems: 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟣𝟥 \mathsf{Thm13} : COM-MONOID ( U { M } , ⋅ ( M × M ) → M , 𝖾 M ) {\textsf{COM-MONOID}(U_{\{M\}},\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}},\mathsf{e}_{M})} 
 x (models of COM-MON define commutative monoids). 
 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟧 \mathsf{Def5} : ≤ M → M → o = λ x , y : M . ∃ z : M . x ⋅ z = y {{\mathsf{\leq}_{{M}\rightarrow{{M}\rightarrow{o}}}}={\lambda\,x,y:M\mathrel{.}\exists\,z:M\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{z}}={y}}} (weak order). 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟣𝟦 \mathsf{Thm14} : ∀ x : M . x ≤ x {\forall\,x:M\mathrel{.}{x}\mathrel{\leq}{x}} (reflexivity). 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟣𝟧 \mathsf{Thm15} : ∀ x , y , z : M . ( x ≤ y ∧ y ≤ z ) ⇒ x ≤ z {\forall\,x,y,z:M\mathrel{.}{({{x}\mathrel{\leq}{y}}\wedge{{y}\mathrel{\leq}{z}})}\Rightarrow{{x}\mathrel{\leq}{z}}} (transitivity). 
 Thm13 states that each model of COM-MON defines a
commutative monoid. Def5 defines a weak (nonstrict) order
that is a pre-order by Thm14 and Thm15 . We could
have put Def5 , Thm14 , and Thm15 in a
development of MON since Thm14 and Thm15 do
not require that ⋅ \cdot is commutative, but we have put these in
 COM-MON instead since ≤ M → M → o {\mathsf{\leq}_{{M}\rightarrow{{M}\rightarrow{o}}}} is more natural for commutative monoids than for
noncommutative monoids.
 Since COM-MON is an extension of MON , there is an
inclusion (i.e., a theory morphism whose mapping is the identity
function) from MON to COM-MON . This inclusion is
defined by the following theory translation definition module:
 Theory Translation Definition 5.3 ( MON to COM-MON ) 
 Name: MON-to-COM-MON . 
 Source theory: MON . 
 Target theory: COM-MON . 
 Base type mapping: 
 1. 
 M ↦ M M\mapsto M . 
 Constant mapping: 
 1. 
 ⋅ ( M × M ) → M ↦ ⋅ ( M × M ) → M {\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}}\mapsto{\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}} . 
 2. 
 𝖾 M ↦ 𝖾 M {\mathsf{e}_{M}}\mapsto{\mathsf{e}_{M}} . 
 MON-to-COM-MON is a theory morphism from MON to
 COM-MON and thus is a development morphism from
 MON-3 to COM-MON-1 . By virtue of this development
morphism, the definitions and theorems of MON-3 can be freely
transported verbatim to COM-MON-1 . In the rest of the paper,
when a theory T ′ T^{\prime} is an extension of a theory T T and D D is a
development of T T , we will assume that that the definitions and
theorems of D D are accessible in any trivial or nontrivial
development of T ′ T^{\prime} without explicitly transporting them via the
inclusion from T T to T ′ T^{\prime} .
 6 Transformation Monoids
 A very important type of monoid is a monoid composed of
transformations of a set. Let s s be a nonempty set. Then
 ( f , ∘ , 𝗂𝖽 ) (f,\circ,\mathsf{id}) , where f f is a set of (partial or total)
functions from s s to s s ,
 ∘ : ( ( s → s ) × ( s → s ) ) → ( s → s ) \circ:((s\rightarrow s)\times(s\rightarrow s))\rightarrow(s\rightarrow s) 
 is function composition, and 𝗂𝖽 : s → s \mathsf{id}:s\rightarrow s is the identity function, is a transformation
monoid on s s if f f is closed under ∘ \circ and 𝗂𝖽 ∈ f \mathsf{id}\in f .
It is easy to verify that every transformation monoid is a monoid. If
 f f contains every function in the function space s → s s\rightarrow s , then
 ( f , ∘ , 𝗂𝖽 ) (f,\circ,\mathsf{id}) is clearly a transformation monoid which is
called the full transformation monoid on s s . Let us say that
a transformation monoid ( f , ∘ , 𝗂𝖽 ) (f,\circ,\mathsf{id}) is standard if
 f f contains only total functions. In many developments, nonstandard
transformation monoids are ignored, but there is no reason to do that
here since Alonzo admits undefined expressions and partial functions.
 Consider the following theory ONE-BT of one base type:
 Theory Definition 6.1 (One Base Type) 
 Name: ONE-BT . 
 Base types: S S . 
 Constants: 
 Axioms: 
 This theory is sufficient for defining the notion of a transformation
monoid in a development of it, but we must first introduce some
general facts about function composition. To do that, we need a
theory FUN-COMP with four base types in order to state the
associativity theorem for function composition in full generality:
 Theory Definition 6.2 (Function Composition) 
 Name: FUN-COMP . 
 Base types: A , B , C , D A,B,C,D . 
 Constants: 
 Axioms: 
 We introduce two theorems for function composition in a development of
 FUN-COMP :
 Development Definition 6.3 (Function Composition 1) 
 Name: FUN-COMP-1 . 
 Bottom theory: FUN-COMP . 
 Definitions and theorems: 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟣𝟨 \mathsf{Thm16} :
 ∀ f : A → B , g : B → C , h : C → D . f ∘ ( g ∘ h ) = ( f ∘ g ) ∘ h {\forall\,f:{A}\rightarrow{B},\,g:{B}\rightarrow{C},\,h:{C}\rightarrow{D}\mathrel{.}{f\circ{({g}\circ{h})}}={({f}\circ{g})\circ{h}}} 
 x ( ∘ \circ is associative). 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟣𝟩 \mathsf{Thm17} :
 ∀ f : A → B . 𝗂𝖽 A → A ∘ f = f ∘ 𝗂𝖽 B → B = f {\forall\,f:{A}\rightarrow{B}\mathrel{.}{\mathsf{id}_{{A}\rightarrow{A}}\circ{f}}\mathrel{=}{f\circ{\mathsf{id}_{{B}\rightarrow{B}}}}\mathrel{=}{f}} 
 x (identity functions are left and right identity elements). 
 The parametric pseudoconstants ∘ ( ( α → β ) × ( β → γ ) ) → ( α → γ ) {\circ_{{({({\alpha}\rightarrow{\beta})}\times{({\beta}\rightarrow{\gamma})})}\rightarrow{({\alpha}\rightarrow{\gamma})}}} and 𝗂𝖽 α → α {\mathsf{id}_{{\alpha}\rightarrow{\alpha}}} are defined in
Table  2 . The infix notation for the application of

Table  2 .

 We can obtain the theorem that all transformation monoids are monoids

 (transformation monoid). 
 Thm20 (Thm4-via-MON-to-ONE-BT) : 
 ∀ s : { S → S } . {\forall\,s:\{{S}\rightarrow{S}\}\mathrel{.}} 
 trans-monoid s ⇒ 𝖬𝖮𝖭𝖮𝖨𝖣 ( s , ∘ ( ( S → S ) × ( S → S ) ) → ( S → S ) ↾ s × s , 𝗂𝖽 S → S ) {{\textsf{trans-monoid}\,s}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{MONOID}(s,{\circ_{{({({S}\rightarrow{S})}\times{({S}\rightarrow{S})})}\rightarrow{({S}\rightarrow{S})}}}\mbox{$\upharpoonright$}\nolinebreak_{{s}\times{s}},\mathsf{id}_{{S}\rightarrow{S}})}} 
 x (transformation monoids are monoids). 
 New target development: ONE-BT-2 . 
 New development morphism: MON-1-to-ONE-BT-2 . 
 trans-monoid is a predicate that is true when it is applied
to a set of functions of S → S {{S}\rightarrow{S}} that forms a transformation
monoid. Thm20 says that every transformation monoid —
including the full transformation monoid — is a monoid.
 7 Monoid Actions
 A (left) monoid action is a structure
 ( m , s , ⋅ , e , 𝖺𝖼𝗍 ) (m,s,\cdot,e,\mathsf{act}) where ( m , ⋅ , e ) (m,\cdot,e) is a monoid and
 𝖺𝖼𝗍 : ( m × s ) → s \mathsf{act}:(m\times s)\rightarrow s is a function such that
 ( 1 ) ​ x 𝖺𝖼𝗍 ( y 𝖺𝖼𝗍 z ) = ( x ⋅ y ) 𝖺𝖼𝗍 z (1)\ x\mathrel{\mathsf{act}}(y\mathrel{\mathsf{act}}z)=(x\cdot y)\mathrel{\mathsf{act}}z 
 for all x , y ∈ M x,y\in M and z ∈ s z\in s 
and
 ( 2 ) ​ e 𝖺𝖼𝗍 z = z (2)\ e\mathrel{\mathsf{act}}z=z 
 for all z ∈ s z\in s . We
say in this case that the monoid ( m , ⋅ , e ) (m,\cdot,e) acts on the set
 s s by the function  𝖺𝖼𝗍 \mathsf{act} .
 Let 𝐙 o \mathbf{Z}_{o} be the formula
 MON-ACTION ​ ( 𝐌 { α } , 𝐒 { β } , 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α , 𝐄 α , 𝐆 ( α × β ) → β ) , \textsf{MON-ACTION}(\mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}},\mathbf{S}_{\{\beta\}},\mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{G}_{{({\alpha}\times{\beta})}\rightarrow{\beta}}), 
 where
 MON-ACTION is the abbreviation introduced by the notational
definition given in Table  3 . 𝐙 o \mathbf{Z}_{o} 
asserts that ( m , s , ⋅ , e , 𝖺𝖼𝗍 ) (m,s,\cdot,e,\mathsf{act}) is a monoid action where m m 
is a set denoting 𝐌 { α } \mathbf{M}_{\{\alpha\}} , s s is a set
denoting 𝐒 { β } \mathbf{S}_{\{\beta\}} , ⋅ : ( m × m ) → m \cdot:(m\times m)\rightarrow m is a function denoting 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α \mathbf{F}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}} , e e is an element of m m denoting
 𝐄 α \mathbf{E}_{\alpha} , and 𝖺𝖼𝗍 : ( m × s ) → s \mathsf{act}:(m\times s)\rightarrow s is a
function denoting 𝐆 ( α × β ) → β \mathbf{G}_{{({\alpha}\times{\beta})}\rightarrow{\beta}} .
 A theory of monoid actions is defined as an extension of the theory of
monoids:
 Theory Extension 7.1 (Monoid Actions) 
 Name: MON-ACT . 
 Extends MON . 
 New base types: S S . 
 New constants: 𝖺𝖼𝗍 ( M × S ) → S \mathsf{act}_{{({M}\times{S})}\rightarrow{S}} . 
 New axioms: 
 3. 
 ∀ x , y : M , s : S . x 𝖺𝖼𝗍 ( y 𝖺𝖼𝗍 s ) = ( x ⋅ y ) 𝖺𝖼𝗍 s {\forall\,x,y:M,\,s:S\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\mathsf{act}}{({y}\mathrel{\mathsf{act}}{s})}}={{({x}\mathrel{\cdot}{y})}\mathrel{\mathsf{act}}{s}}} 
 x ( 𝖺𝖼𝗍 \mathsf{act} is compatible with ⋅ \cdot ). 
 4. 
 ∀ s : S . 𝖾 𝖺𝖼𝗍 s = s {\forall\,s:S\mathrel{.}{{\mathsf{e}}\mathrel{\mathsf{act}}{s}}={s}} ( 𝖺𝖼𝗍 \mathsf{act} is compatible with
 𝖾 \mathsf{e} ). 
 We begin a development of MON-ACT by adding the definitions
and theorems below:
 Development Definition 7.2 (Monoid Actions 1) 
 Name: MON-ACT-1 . 
 Bottom theory: MON-ACT . 
 Definitions and theorems: 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟤𝟣 \mathsf{Thm21} : MON-ACTION ( U { M } , U { S } , ⋅ ( M × M ) → M , 𝖾 M , 𝖺𝖼𝗍 ( M × S ) → S ) {\textsf{MON-ACTION}(U_{\{M\}},U_{\{S\}},\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}},\mathsf{e}_{M},\mathsf{act}_{{({M}\times{S})}\rightarrow{S}})} 
 x (models of MON-ACT define monoid actions). 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟤𝟤 \mathsf{Thm22} : 𝖳𝖮𝖳𝖠𝖫 ⁡ ( 𝖺𝖼𝗍 ( M × S ) → S ) {\mathsf{TOTAL}(\mathsf{act}_{{({M}\times{S})}\rightarrow{S}})} ( 𝖺𝖼𝗍 \mathsf{act} is total). 
 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟩 \mathsf{Def7} : 𝗈𝗋𝖻𝗂𝗍 S → { S } = λ s : S . { t : S ∣ ∃ x : M . x 𝖺𝖼𝗍 s = t } {{\mathsf{orbit}_{{S}\rightarrow{\{S\}}}}={\lambda\,s:S\mathrel{.}\{{{t}:{S}}\mid{\exists\,x:M\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\mathsf{act}}{s}}={t}}\}}} (orbit). 
 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟪 \mathsf{Def8} : 𝗌𝗍𝖺𝖻𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗓𝖾𝗋 S → { M } = λ s : S . { x : M ∣ x 𝖺𝖼𝗍 s = s } {{\mathsf{stabilizer}_{{S}\rightarrow{\{M\}}}}={\lambda\,s:S\mathrel{.}\{{{x}:{M}}\mid{{{x}\mathrel{\mathsf{act}}{s}}={s}}\}}} (stabilizer). 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟤𝟥 \mathsf{Thm23} : ∀ s : S . 𝗌𝗎𝖻𝗆𝗈𝗇𝗈𝗂𝖽 ⁡ ( 𝗌𝗍𝖺𝖻𝗂𝗅𝗂𝗓𝖾𝗋 ​ s ) {\forall\,s:S\mathrel{.}\mathsf{submonoid}\,(\mathsf{stabilizer}\,s)} 
 (stabilizers are submonoids). 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟤𝟣 \mathsf{Thm21} states that each model of MON-ACTION defines
a monoid action. 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟤𝟤 \mathsf{Thm22} says that 𝖺𝖼𝗍 ( M × S ) → S \mathsf{act}_{{({M}\times{S})}\rightarrow{S}} is total. 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟩 \mathsf{Def7} and
 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟪 \mathsf{Def8} introduce the concepts of an orbit and a stabilizer.
And 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟤𝟥 \mathsf{Thm23} states that a stabilizer of a monoid action
 ( m , s , ⋅ , e , 𝖺𝖼𝗍 ) (m,s,\cdot,e,\mathsf{act}) is a submonoid of the monoid
 ( m , ⋅ , e ) (m,\cdot,e) . The power of this machinery — monoid actions with
orbits and stabilizers — is low with arbitrary monoids but very high
with groups, i.e., monoids in which every element has an inverse.
 Monoid actions are common in monoid theory. We will present two
important examples of monoid actions. The first is the monoid action
 ( m , m , ⋅ , e , ⋅ ) (m,m,\cdot,e,\cdot) such that the monoid ( m , ⋅ , e ) (m,\cdot,e) acts on the
set m m of its elements by its function ⋅ \cdot . We formalize this by
creating the theory morphism from MON-ACT to MON 
that maps
 ( M , S , ⋅ ( M × M ) → M , 𝐞 M , 𝖺𝖼𝗍 ( M × S ) → S ) (M,S,{\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}},{\mathbf{e}_{M}},{\mathsf{act}_{{({M}\times{S})}\rightarrow{S}}}) 
 to
 ( M , M , ⋅ ( M × M ) → M , 𝐞 M , ⋅ ( M × M ) → M ) : (M,M,{\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}},{\mathbf{e}_{M}},{\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}}):

 8 Monoid Homomorphisms
 Roughly speaking, a monoid homomorphism is a
structure-preserving mapping from one monoid to another.
 Let 𝐖 o \mathbf{W}_{o} be the formula
 MON-HOMOM ​ ( 𝐌 { α } 1 , 𝐌 { β } 2 , 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α 1 , 𝐄 α 1 , 𝐅 ( β × β ) → β 2 , 𝐄 β 2 , 𝐇 α → β ) , {\textsf{MON-HOMOM}(\mathbf{M}^{1}_{\{\alpha\}},\mathbf{M}^{2}_{\{\beta\}},\mathbf{F}^{1}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}},\mathbf{E}^{1}_{\alpha},\mathbf{F}^{2}_{{({\beta}\times{\beta})}\rightarrow{\beta}},\mathbf{E}^{2}_{\beta},\mathbf{H}_{{\alpha}\rightarrow{\beta}})}, 
 where MON-HOMOM is the abbreviation introduced by the
notational definition given in Table  3 .
 𝐖 o \mathbf{W}_{o} asserts that
 ( m 1 , m 2 , ⋅ 1 , e 1 , ⋅ 2 , e 2 , h ) (m_{1},m_{2},\cdot_{1},e_{1},\cdot_{2},e_{2},h) 
 is a structure, where m 1 m_{1} is a set denoting 𝐌 { α } 1 \mathbf{M}^{1}_{\{\alpha\}} , m 2 m_{2} is a set denoting 𝐌 { α } 2 \mathbf{M}^{2}_{\{\alpha\}} , ⋅ 1 : ( m 1 × m 1 ) → m 1 \cdot_{1}:(m_{1}\times m_{1})\rightarrow m_{1} is a function
denoting 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α 1 {\mathbf{F}^{1}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}}} , e 1 e_{1} is an element of m 1 m_{1} denoting
 𝐄 α 1 {\mathbf{E}^{1}_{\alpha}} , ⋅ 2 : ( m 2 × m 2 ) → m 2 \cdot_{2}:(m_{2}\times m_{2})\rightarrow m_{2} 
is a function denoting 𝐅 ( α × α ) → α 2 {\mathbf{F}^{2}_{{({\alpha}\times{\alpha})}\rightarrow{\alpha}}} , e 2 e_{2} is an element of m 2 m_{2} denoting
 𝐄 α 2 {\mathbf{E}^{2}_{\alpha}} , and h : m 1 → m 2 h:m_{1}\rightarrow m_{2} is a function
denoting 𝐇 α → β {\mathbf{H}_{{\alpha}\rightarrow{\beta}}} , such that h h is a
monoid homomorphism from a monoid ( m 1 , ⋅ 1 , e 1 ) (m_{1},\cdot_{1},e_{1}) to a monoid
 ( m 2 , ⋅ 2 , e 2 ) (m_{2},\cdot_{2},e_{2}) .
 The notion of a monoid homomorphism is captured in the theory
 MON-HOM :
 Theory Definition 8.1 (Monoid Homomorphisms) 
 Name: MON-HOM . 
 Base types: M 1 , M 2 M_{1},M_{2} . 
 Constants: ⋅ ( M 1 × M 1 ) → M 1 , 𝖾 M 1 , ⋅ ( M 2 × M 2 ) → M 2 , 𝖾 M 2 , 𝗁 M 1 → M 2 \cdot_{{({M_{1}}\times{M_{1}})}\rightarrow{M_{1}}},\;\mathsf{e}_{M_{1}},\;\cdot_{{({M_{2}}\times{M_{2}})}\rightarrow{M_{2}}},\;\mathsf{e}_{M_{2}},\;\mathsf{h}_{{M_{1}}\rightarrow{M_{2}}} . 
 Axioms: 
 1. 
 ∀ x , y , z : M 1 . x ⋅ ( y ⋅ z ) = ( x ⋅ y ) ⋅ z {\forall\,x,y,z:M_{1}\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{({y}\mathrel{\cdot}{z})}}={{({x}\mathrel{\cdot}{y})}\mathrel{\cdot}{z}}} 
 ( ⋅ ( M 1 × M 1 ) → M 1 \cdot_{{({M_{1}}\times{M_{1}})}\rightarrow{M_{1}}} is associative). 
 2. 
 ∀ x : M 1 . 𝖾 ⋅ x = x ⋅ 𝖾 = x {\forall\,x:M_{1}\mathrel{.}{{\mathsf{e}}\mathrel{\cdot}{x}}\mathrel{=}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{\mathsf{e}}}\mathrel{=}{x}} ( 𝖾 M 1 \mathsf{e}_{M_{1}} is an identity element). 
 3. 
 ∀ x , y , z : M 2 . x ⋅ ( y ⋅ z ) = ( x ⋅ y ) ⋅ z {\forall\,x,y,z:M_{2}\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{({y}\mathrel{\cdot}{z})}}={{({x}\mathrel{\cdot}{y})}\mathrel{\cdot}{z}}} 
 ( ⋅ ( M 2 × M 2 ) → M 2 \cdot_{{({M_{2}}\times{M_{2}})}\rightarrow{M_{2}}} is associative). 
 4. 
 ∀ x : M 2 . 𝖾 ⋅ x = x ⋅ 𝖾 = x {\forall\,x:M_{2}\mathrel{.}{{\mathsf{e}}\mathrel{\cdot}{x}}\mathrel{=}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{\mathsf{e}}}\mathrel{=}{x}} ( 𝖾 M 2 \mathsf{e}_{M_{2}} is an identity element). 
 5. 
 ∀ x , y : M 1 . 𝗁 ⁡ ( x ⋅ y ) = ( 𝗁 ​ x ) ⋅ ( 𝗁 ​ y ) {\forall\,x,y:M_{1}\mathrel{.}{\mathsf{h}\,({x}\mathrel{\cdot}{y})}={{(\mathsf{h}\,x)}\mathrel{\cdot}{(\mathsf{h}\,y)}}} 
 (first homomorphism property). 
 6. 
 𝗁 ​ 𝖾 M 1 = 𝖾 M 2 {{\mathsf{h}\,\mathsf{e}_{M_{1}}}={\mathsf{e}_{M_{2}}}} 
 (second homomorphism property). 
 𝗁 M 1 → M 2 \mathsf{h}_{{M_{1}}\rightarrow{M_{2}}} denotes a monoid homomorphism from
the monoid denoted by
 ( M 1 , ⋅ ( M 1 × M 1 ) → M 1 , 𝖾 M 1 ) (M_{1},\cdot_{{({M_{1}}\times{M_{1}})}\rightarrow{M_{1}}},\mathsf{e}_{M_{1}}) 
 to the monoid denoted by
 ( M 2 , ⋅ ( M 2 × M 2 ) → M 2 , 𝖾 M 2 ) . (M_{2},\cdot_{{({M_{2}}\times{M_{2}})}\rightarrow{M_{2}}},\mathsf{e}_{M_{2}}).

 to the
monoid denoted by
 ( { M } , ⊙ ( { M } × { M } ) → { M } , 𝖤 { M } ) (\{M\},\odot_{{({\{M\}}\times{\{M\}})}\rightarrow{\{M\}}},\mathsf{E}_{\{M\}}) 
 is the function that maps a member x x of the denotation of M M to the
singleton  { x } \{x\} . This monoid homomorphism is formalized by the
following development morphism:
 Development Translation Definition 8.5 ( MON-HOM to MON )

 2.

 3.

 It is a straightforward exercise to verify that
 HOM-MON-to-MON-4 is a theory morphism.
 We can now transport Thm26 from MON-HOM to

monoid homomorphism:

 9 Monoids over Real Number Arithmetic
 We need machinery concerning real number arithmetic to express some
concepts about monoids. For instance, an iterated product operator
for monoids involves integers. To formalize these kinds of concepts,
we need a theory of monoids that includes real number arithmetic. We
present in  [ 20 , Chapter 12] COF , a theory of
complete ordered fields. COF is categorical in the standard
sense (see  [ 20 ] ). That is, it has a single standard model
up to isomorphism that defines the structure of real number
arithmetic.
 We define a theory of monoids over COF by extending
 COF with the language and axioms of MON :
 Theory Extension 9.1 (Monoids over COF ) 
 Name: MON-over-COF . 
 Extends COF . 
 New base types: M M . 
 New constants: ⋅ ( M × M ) → M , 𝖾 M \cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}},\;\mathsf{e}_{M} . 
 New axioms: 
 19. 
 ∀ x , y , z : M . x ⋅ ( y ⋅ z ) = ( x ⋅ y ) ⋅ z {\forall\,x,y,z:M\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{({y}\mathrel{\cdot}{z})}}={{({x}\mathrel{\cdot}{y})}\mathrel{\cdot}{z}}} 
 ( ⋅ \cdot is associative). 
 20. 
 ∀ x : M . 𝖾 ⋅ x = x ⋅ 𝖾 = x {\forall\,x:M\mathrel{.}{{\mathsf{e}}\mathrel{\cdot}{x}}\mathrel{=}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{\mathsf{e}}}\mathrel{=}{x}} ( 𝖾 \mathsf{e} is an identity element). 
 We can now define an iterated product operator for monoids in a
development of MON-over-COF-1 :

 x (extended iterated product).

operator. Notice that we are utilizing the special notation
 ( ∏ 𝐢 = 𝐌 R 𝐍 R 𝐀 M ) \bigg(\prod\limits_{{\mathbf{i}}={\mathbf{M}_{R}}}^{\mathbf{N}_{R}}{\mathbf{A}_{M}}\bigg) 
 for the iterated product defined in

 10 Monoid Theory Applied to Strings
 In this section we will show how we can apply the machinery of our
monoid theory formalization to a theory of strings over an abstract
alphabet. A string over an alphabet A A is a finite sequence of
values from A A . The finite sequence s s can be represented as a
partial function s : ℕ → A s:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow A such that, for some n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N} ,
 s ⁡ ( m ) s(m) is defined iff m < n m<n .
 In  [ 20 , Chapter 10] we introduce compact notation for
finite (and infinite) sequences represented in this manner. The
notation requires a system of natural numbers as defined
in  [ 20 ] . The development COF-dev-2 of the
theory COF presented in  [ 20 , Chapter 12] includes
a system of natural numbers. Therefore, we can define a theory of
strings as an extension of COF plus a base type A A that
represents an abstract alphabet:
 Theory Extension 10.1 (Strings) 
 Name: STR . 
 Extends COF . 
 New base types: A A . 
 New constants: 
 New axioms: 
 Since STR is an extension of COF , we can assume that
 STR-1 is a development of STR that contains the 7
definitions of COF-dev-2 named as COF-Def1 , …,
 COF-Def7 and the 22 theorems of COF-dev-2 named as
 COF-Thm1 , …, COF-Thm22 . We can extend
 STR-1 as follows to include the basic definitions and
theorems of strings:
 Development Extension 10.2 (Strings 2) 
 Name: STR-2 . 
 Extends STR-1 . 
 New definitions and theorems: 
 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟣𝟢 \mathsf{Def10} :
 𝗌𝗍𝗋 { R → A } = [ A ] {{\mathsf{str}_{\{{R}\rightarrow{A}\}}}={[A]}} 
 (string quasitype). 
 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟣𝟣 \mathsf{Def11} :
 ϵ R → A = [ ] R → A {{\epsilon_{{R}\rightarrow{A}}}={{[\;]}_{{R}\rightarrow{A}}}} (empty string). 
 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟣𝟤 \mathsf{Def12} :
 𝖼𝖺𝗍 ( ( R → A ) × ( R → A ) ) → ( R → A ) = {{\mathsf{cat}_{{({({R}\rightarrow{A})}\times{({R}\rightarrow{A})})}\rightarrow{({R}\rightarrow{A})}}}={}} 
 I ​ f : ( 𝗌𝗍𝗋 × 𝗌𝗍𝗋 ) → 𝗌𝗍𝗋 . {\mathrm{I}\,f:{({\mathsf{str}}\times{\mathsf{str}})}\rightarrow{\mathsf{str}}\mathrel{.}} 
 ∀ x : 𝗌𝗍𝗋 . f ⁡ ( ϵ , x ) = x ∧ {{\forall\,x:\mathsf{str}\mathrel{.}{f\,(\epsilon,x)}={x}}\wedge{}} 
 ∀ a : A , x , y : 𝗌𝗍𝗋 . f ( a : : x , y ) = a : : f ( x , y ) {\forall\,a:A,\,x,y:\mathsf{str}\mathrel{.}{f\,({a}::{x},y)}={{a}::{f\,(x,y)}}} .
 (concatenation). 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟥𝟥 \mathsf{Thm33} :
 ∀ x : 𝗌𝗍𝗋 . ϵ ​ x = x ​ ϵ = x {\forall\,x:\mathsf{str}\mathrel{.}{{\epsilon}{x}}\mathrel{=}{{x}{\epsilon}}\mathrel{=}{x}} 
 ( ϵ \epsilon is an identity element). 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟥𝟦 \mathsf{Thm34} :
 ∀ x , y , z : 𝗌𝗍𝗋 . x ⁡ ( y ​ z ) = ( x ​ y ) ​ z {\forall\,x,y,z:\mathsf{str}\mathrel{.}{{x}{({y}{z})}}={{({x}{y})}{z}}} 
 ( 𝖼𝖺𝗍 \mathsf{cat} is associative). 
 Def10–Def12 utilize the compact notation introduced in
Table 10.1 in  [ 20 ] and Thm33–Thm34 utilize the
compact notation introduced in Table  1 .
 We can define a development translation from MON-over-COF to
 STR-2 as follows:
 Development Translation Definition 10.3 ( MON-over-COF to STR-2 ) 
 Name: MON-over-COF-to-STR-2 . 
 Source development: MON-over-COF . 
 Target development: STR-2 . 
 Base type mapping: 
 1. 
 R ↦ R R\mapsto R . 
 2. 
 M ↦ 𝗌𝗍𝗋 { R → A } M\mapsto{\mathsf{str}_{\{{R}\rightarrow{A}\}}} . 
 Constant mapping: 
 1. 
 0 R ↦ 0 R 0_{R}\mapsto 0_{R} . 
 ⋮ \vdots 
 10. 
 𝗅𝗎𝖻 R → { R } → o ↦ 𝗅𝗎𝖻 R → { R } → o \mathsf{lub}_{{R}\rightarrow{{\{R\}}\rightarrow{o}}}\mapsto\mathsf{lub}_{{R}\rightarrow{{\{R\}}\rightarrow{o}}} . 
 11. 
 ⋅ ( M × M ) → M ↦ 𝖼𝖺𝗍 ( ( R → A ) × ( R → A ) ) → ( R → A ) {\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}}\mapsto{\mathsf{cat}_{{({({R}\rightarrow{A})}\times{({R}\rightarrow{A})})}\rightarrow{({R}\rightarrow{A})}}} . 
 12. 
 𝖾 M ↦ ϵ R → A {\mathsf{e}_{M}}\mapsto{\epsilon_{{R}\rightarrow{A}}} . 
 MON-over-COF-to-STR-2 has one obligation of the first kind
for the mapped base type M M , which is clearly valid since
 𝗌𝗍𝗋 { R → A } \mathsf{str}_{\{{R}\rightarrow{A}\}} is nonempty.
 MON-over-COF-to-STR-2 has 12 obligations of the second kind
for the 12 mapped constants. The first 10 are trivially valid. The
last 2 are valid by 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟣𝟤 \mathsf{Def12} and 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟣𝟣 \mathsf{Def11} ,
respectively. And MON-over-COF-to-STR-2 has 20 obligations
of the third kind for the 20 axioms of MON-over-COF . The
first 18 are trivially valid. The last 2 are valid by
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟥𝟦 \mathsf{Thm34} and 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟥𝟥 \mathsf{Thm33} , respectively. Therefore,
 MON-over-COF-to-STR-2 is a development morphism from the
theory MON-over-COF to the development STR-2 by the
Morphism Theorem [ 20 , Theorem 13.13] .

 11 Related Work
 As we have seen, a theory (or development) graph provides an effective
architecture for formalizing a body of mathematical knowledge. It is
especially useful for creating a large library of formal mathematical
knowledge that, by necessity, must be constructed in parallel by
multiple developers. The library is built in parts by separate
development teams and then the parts are linked together by morphisms.
Mathematical knowledge is organized as a theory graph in several proof
assistants and logical frameworks including Ergo  [ 35 ] ,
IMPS  [ 21 ] , Isabelle  [ 5 ] ,
LF  [ 44 ] , MMT  [ 43 ] , and
PVS  [ 38 ] . Theory graphs are also employed in several
software specification and development systems including
ASL  [ 47 ] , CASL  [ 3 , 4 ] , EHDM  [ 45 ] ,
Hets  [ 32 ] , IOTA  [ 33 ] ,
KIDS  [ 48 ] , OBJ  [ 24 ] , and
Specware  [ 49 ] .
 Simple type theory in the form of Church’s type theory is a popular
logic for formal mathematics. There are several proof assistants that
implement versions of Church’s type theory including
HOL  [ 26 ] , HOL Light  [ 27 ] ,
IMPS  [ 22 ] , Isabelle/HOL  [ 39 ] ,
ProofPower  [ 42 ] , PVS  [ 37 ] , and
TPS  [ 2 ] . As we mentioned in Section  1 ,
the IMPS proof assistant is especially noteworthy here since it
implements LUTINS  [ 13 , 14 , 15 ] , a version of
Church’s type theory that admits undefined expressions and is closely
related to Alonzo.
 In recent years, there has been growing interest in formalizing
mathematics within dependent logics. Several proof assistants and
programming languages are based on versions of dependent type theory
including Agda  [ 7 , 36 ] ,
Automath  [ 34 ] , Coq  [ 50 ] ,
Epigram  [ 11 ] , F ∗ {\rm F}^{\ast}   [ 12 ] ,
Idris  [ 29 ] , Lean  [ 10 ] , and
Nuprl  [ 9 ] . So which type theory is better for formal
mathematics, simple type theory or dependent type theory? This
question has become hotly contested. We hope that the reader will
see our formalization of monoid theory in Alonzo as evidence for the
efficacy of simple type theory as a logical basis for formal
mathematics. The reader might also be interested in looking at these
recent papers that advocate for simple type theory:  [ 6 , 40 , 41 ] .
 Since monoid theory is a relatively simple subject, there have not
been many attempts to formalize it by itself, but there have been
several formalizations of group theory. Here are some
examples:  [ 23 , 25 , 30 , 46 , 51 , 52 ] .
 12 Conclusion
 The developments and development morphisms presented in
Sections  2 – 10 form the development
graph G mon G_{\rm mon} shown in Figure  1 . The
development graph shows all the development morphisms that we have
explicitly defined as well as all inclusions corresponding to theory
extensions we have explicitly defined. A development morphism that is
an inclusion is designated by a ↪ \hookrightarrow arrow and a
noninclusion is designated by a → \rightarrow arrow. There are many,
many more useful development morphisms that are not shown in G mon G_{\rm mon} , including implicit inclusions and a vast number of development
morphisms into the theory COF .
 MON-5 
 COM- MON-1 
 MON- HOM-1 
 COM-MON- over-COF-1 
 MON- over-COF-1 
 MON- ACT-1 
 ONE- BT-2 
 FUN- COMP-1 
 ONE-BT- with-SC-1 
 COF 
 COM- MON-ACT- over-COF-1 
 STR-3 
 Figure 1: The Monoid Theory Development Graph 
 The construction of G mon G_{\rm mon} illustrates how a body of
mathematical knowledge can be formalized in Alonzo as a development
graph in accordance with the little theories method. G mon G_{\rm mon} 
could be extended to include other mathematical concepts related to
monoids such as categories. It could be incorporated in a development
graph that formalizes a more extensive body of mathematical knowledge.
And it could also be used as a foundation for building a formalization
of group theory. This would be done by lifting each development D D 
of a theory T T that extends MON to a development D ′ D^{\prime} of a
theory T ′ T^{\prime} that extends a theory GRP obtained by adding an
inverse operation to MON . The lifting of D D to D ′ D^{\prime} would
include constructing inclusions from MON to GRP and
from T T to  T ′ T^{\prime} .
 The formalization of monoid theory we have presented demonstrates
three things. First, it demonstrates the power of the little theories
method. The formalization is largely free of redundancy since each
mathematical topic is articulated in just one development D D , the
development for the little theory that is optimal for the topic in
level of abstraction and choice of vocabulary. If we create a
translation Φ \Phi from D D to another development D ′ D^{\prime} and prove that
 Φ \Phi is a morphism, then we can freely transport the definitions and
theorems of D D to D ′ D^{\prime} via Φ \Phi . That is, an abstract concept or
fact that has been validated in D D can be translated to a concrete
instance of the concept or fact that is automatically validated in
 D ′ D^{\prime} provided the translation is a morphism. (This is illustrated by
our use of the development morphism MON-over-COF-to-STR-2 to
transport definitions and theorems about monoids to a development
about strings.) As the result, the same concept or fact can appear in
many places in the theory graph but under different assumptions and
involving different vocabulary. (For example, the notion of a
submonoid represented by the constant 𝗌𝗎𝖻𝗆𝗈𝗇𝗈𝗂𝖽 { M } → o {\mathsf{submonoid}_{{\{M\}}\rightarrow{o}}} defined in MON-1 appears in
 ONE-BT-2 as the notion of a transformation monoid represented
by the constant trans-monoid { S → S } → o {\textsf{trans-monoid}_{{\{{S}\rightarrow{S}\}}\rightarrow{o}}} .)
 Second, the formalization demonstrates that Alonzo is well suited for
expressing and reasoning about mathematical ideas. The simple type
theory machinery of Alonzo — function and product types, function
application and abstraction, definite description, and ordered pairs
— enables mathematical expressions to be formulated in a direct and
natural manner. It also enables almost every single mathematical
structure or set of mathematical structures to be specified by an
Alonzo development. (For example, the development ONE-BT-2 
specifies the set of structures consisting of a set S S and the set S → S S\rightarrow S of transformations on S S .) The admission of undefined
expressions in Alonzo enables statements involving partial and total
functions and definite descriptions to be expressed directly,
naturally, and succinctly. (For example, if M = ( m , ⋅ , e ) M=(m,\cdot,e) is a
monoid, the operation that makes a submonoid m ′ ⊆ m m^{\prime}\subseteq m of M M a
monoid itself is exactly what is expected: the partial function that
results from restricting ⋅ \cdot to m ′ × m ′ m^{\prime}\times m^{\prime} .) And the
notational definitions and conventions employed in Alonzo enables
mathematical expressions to be presented with the same notation that
is used mathematical practice. (For example, Thm33 :
 ∀ x : 𝗌𝗍𝗋 . ϵ ​ x = x ​ ϵ = x {\forall\,x:\mathsf{str}\mathrel{.}{{\epsilon}{x}}\mathrel{=}{{x}{\epsilon}}\mathrel{=}{x}} , that states
 ϵ \epsilon is an identity element for concatenation, is written just
as one would expect it to be written in mathematical practice.)
 Third, the formalization demonstrates that the communication-oriented
approach to formal mathematics (with traditional proofs) has two
advantages over the certification-oriented approach (with formal
proofs): (1) communication is more effective and (2) formalization is
easier. (These two approaches are defined in
Section  1 .) The certification-oriented approach is done
with the help of a proof assistant and all proofs are formal and
mechanically checked. Proof assistants are consequently very complex
and notoriously difficult to learn how to use. Traditional proofs are
easier to read and write than formal proofs and are better suited for
communicating the ideas behind proofs. Moreover, since the
communication-oriented approach does not require a facility for
developing and checking formal proofs, it can be done with software
support that is much simpler and easier to use than a proof assistant.
(In this paper, our software support was just a set of LaTeX macros
and environments.)
 The great majority of mathematics practitioners — including
mathematicians — are much more interested in communicating
mathematical ideas than in formally certifying mathematical results.
Hence, the communication-oriented approach is likely to serve the
needs of the average mathematics practitioner much better than the
certification-oriented approach. This is especially true when the
mathematical knowledge involved is well understood (such as monoid
theory) and certification via traditional proof is adequate for the
purpose at hand.
 Appendix A Validation of Definitions and Theorems
 Let D = ( T , Ξ ) D=(T,\Xi) be a development where T T is the bottom theory of
the development and Ξ = [ P 1 , … , P n ] \Xi=[P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}] is the list of
definition and theorem packages of the development. For each i i with
 1 ≤ i ≤ n 1\leq i\leq n , P i P_{i} has the form ( p , 𝐜 α , 𝐀 α , π ) (p,{{\mathbf{c}}_{\alpha}},\mathbf{A}_{\alpha},\pi) if P i P_{i} is a definition package
and has the form P i = ( p , 𝐀 o , π ) P_{i}=(p,\mathbf{A}_{o},\pi) if P i P_{i} is a
theorem package. Define T 0 = T T_{0}=T and, for all i i with 0 ≤ i ≤ n − 1 0\leq i\leq n-1 , define T i + 1 = T ⁡ [ P i + 1 ] T_{i+1}=T[P_{i+1}] if P i + 1 P_{i+1} is a definition
package and T i + 1 = T i T_{i+1}=T_{i} if P i + 1 P_{i+1} is a theorem package. In the
former case, π i + 1 \pi_{i+1} is a proof that 𝐀 α ↓ \mathbf{A}_{\alpha}{\downarrow} 
is valid in T i T_{i} , and in the latter case, π i + 1 \pi_{i+1} is a proof that
 𝐀 o \mathbf{A}_{o} is valid in T i T_{i} . These proofs may be either
traditional or formal.
 The validation proofs for the definitions and theorems of a
development are not included in the modules we have used to construct
developments and to transport definitions and theorems. Instead, we
give in this appendix, for each of the definitions and theorems in the
developments defined in Sections  2 – 10 ,
a traditional proof that validates the definition or theorem. The
proofs are almost entirely straightforward. The proofs reference some
of the axioms, rules of inference, and metatheorems of 𝔄 \mathfrak{A} ,
the formal proof system for Alonzo presented in  [ 20 ] .
These are legitimate to use since 𝔄 \mathfrak{A} is sound by the
Soundness Theorem [ 20 , Theorem 8.6 and Theorem B.11] .
 A.1 Development of MON

 x (models of MON define monoids).

 (2) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma

 3. 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟥 \mathsf{Thm3} : ∀ x : M . ( ∀ y : M . x ⋅ y = y ⋅ x = y ) ⇒ x = 𝖾 {\forall\,x:M\mathrel{.}{(\forall\,y:M\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{y}}\mathrel{=}{{y}\mathrel{\cdot}{x}}\mathrel{=}{y})}\Rightarrow{{x}={\mathsf{e}}}}

 4.

 5. 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟦 \mathsf{Thm4} : ∀ s : { M } . 𝗌𝗎𝖻𝗆𝗈𝗇𝗈𝗂𝖽 s ⇒ 𝖬𝖮𝖭𝖮𝖨𝖣 ( s , ⋅ ↾ s × s , 𝖾 ) {\forall\,s:\{M\}\mathrel{.}{\mathsf{submonoid}\,s}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{MONOID}(s,\cdot\mbox{$\upharpoonright$}\nolinebreak_{{s}\times{s}},\mathsf{e})}} 
 x (submonoids are
monoids).
 Proof of the theorem. 
Let A o \textbf{A}_{o} be

 and T = ( L , Γ ) T=(L,\Gamma) be MON extended by 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟣 \mathsf{Def1} . We

 Γ ∪ { A o } \displaystyle\Gamma\cup\{\textbf{A}_{o}\}

 Γ ∪ { A o } \displaystyle\Gamma\cup\{\textbf{A}_{o}\}

 (3)

 (5) 
 Γ ∪ { A o } \displaystyle\Gamma\cup\{\textbf{A}_{o}\} 
 ⊨ ∀ x , y , z : s . ⋅ ↾ ( s × s ) ( x , ⋅ ↾ ( s × s ) ( y , z ) ) \displaystyle\vDash\forall\,x,y,z:s\mathrel{.}\cdot\mbox{$\upharpoonright$}_{({s}\times{s})}\,(x,\cdot\mbox{$\upharpoonright$}_{({s}\times{s})}\,(y,z)) 
 = ⋅ ↾ ( s × s ) ( ⋅ ↾ ( s × s ) ( x , y ) , z ) \displaystyle\hskip 8.61108pt=\cdot\mbox{$\upharpoonright$}_{({s}\times{s})}\,(\cdot\mbox{$\upharpoonright$}_{({s}\times{s})}\,(x,y),z)

 Γ ∪ { A o } \displaystyle\Gamma\cup\{\textbf{A}_{o}\}

 (7) 
 Γ ∪ { A o } \displaystyle\Gamma\cup\{\textbf{A}_{o}\}

 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ ∀ s : { M } . A o ⇒ 𝖬𝖮𝖭𝖮𝖨𝖣 ( s , ⋅ ↾ ( s × s ) , 𝖾 ) \displaystyle\vDash\forall\,s:\{M\}\mathrel{.}{\textbf{A}_{o}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{MONOID}(s,\cdot\mbox{$\upharpoonright$}_{({s}\times{s})},\mathsf{e})}

 (1) follows from variables always being defined by  [ 20 , Axiom A5.1] ;

Table  3 ;
(9) follows from (8) by the Deduction Theorem [ 20 , Theorem A.50] ;
and (10) follows from (9) by Universal Generalization [ 20 , Theorem A.30] 
using the fact that ( s : { M } ) ({s}:{\{M\}}) is not free in Γ \Gamma 
since Γ \Gamma is a set of sentences. Therefore, ( ⋆ ) (\star) holds.
 □ \Box 
 6. 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟧 \mathsf{Thm5} : 𝗌𝗎𝖻𝗆𝗈𝗇𝗈𝗂𝖽 ​ { 𝖾 } {\mathsf{submonoid}\,\{\mathsf{e}\}} (minimum submonoid).
 Proof of the theorem. 
Let T = ( L , Γ ) T=(L,\Gamma) be 𝖬𝖮𝖭 \mathsf{MON} extended by 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟣 \mathsf{Def1} . We
must show ( ⋆ ) (\star) T ⊨ 𝗌𝗎𝖻𝗆𝗈𝗇𝗈𝗂𝖽 ​ { 𝖾 } T\vDash{\mathsf{submonoid}\,\{\mathsf{e}\}} .
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ 𝖾 ∈ { 𝖾 } \displaystyle\vDash{\mathsf{e}}\in{\{\mathsf{e}\}} 
 (1) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ { 𝖾 } ≠ ∅ { M } \displaystyle\vDash\{\mathsf{e}\}\not=\emptyset_{\{M\}} 
 (2) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ 𝖾 ⋅ 𝖾 = 𝖾 \displaystyle\vDash\mathsf{e}\cdot\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{e} 
 (3) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ ⋅ ↾ { 𝖾 } × { 𝖾 } ↓ ( { 𝖾 } × { 𝖾 } ) → { 𝖾 } \displaystyle\vDash{\cdot\mbox{$\upharpoonright$}_{{\{\mathsf{e}\}}\times{\{\mathsf{e}\}}}}\downarrow{{({\{\mathsf{e}\}}\times{\{\mathsf{e}\}})}\rightarrow{\{\mathsf{e}\}}} 
 (4) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ 𝗌𝗎𝖻𝗆𝗈𝗇𝗈𝗂𝖽 ​ { 𝖾 } \displaystyle\vDash{\mathsf{submonoid}\,\{\mathsf{e}\}} 
 (5) 
 (1) is trivial;
(2) follows from (1) because { 𝖾 } \{\mathsf{e}\} has at least one member;
(3) follows from Axiom 2 of T T by Universal Instantiation
 [ 20 , Theorem A.14] ;
(4) follows directly from (1), (3), and the fact that the only member of
 { 𝖾 } \{\mathsf{e}\} is 𝖾 \mathsf{e} ; and
(5) follows from (1), (2), (4), and 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟣 \mathsf{Def1} .
Therefore, ( ⋆ ) (\star) holds.
 □ \Box 
 7. 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟨 \mathsf{Thm6} : 𝗌𝗎𝖻𝗆𝗈𝗇𝗈𝗂𝖽 ​ U { M } {\mathsf{submonoid}\,U_{\{M\}}} (maximum submonoid).

We must show ( ⋆ ) (\star) T ⊨ 𝗌𝗎𝖻𝗆𝗈𝗇𝗈𝗂𝖽 ​ U { M } T\vDash{\mathsf{submonoid}\,U_{\{M\}}} .
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ 𝖬𝖮𝖭𝖮𝖨𝖣 ( U { M } , ⋅ ( M × M ) → M , 𝖾 ) \displaystyle\vDash\mathsf{MONOID}(U_{\{M\}},\cdot_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}},\mathsf{e}) 
 (1) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ U { M } ≠ ∅ { M } ∧ 𝖾 ∈ U { M } \displaystyle\vDash U_{\{M\}}\not=\emptyset_{\{M\}}\land\mathsf{e}\in U_{\{M\}} 
 (2) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ ⋅ ↾ U { M } × U { M } ↓ ( U { M } × U { M } ) → U { M } \displaystyle\vDash{\cdot\mbox{$\upharpoonright$}_{{U_{\{M\}}}\times{U_{\{M\}}}}}\downarrow{{({U_{\{M\}}}\times{U_{\{M\}}})}\rightarrow{U_{\{M\}}}} 
 (3) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ 𝗌𝗎𝖻𝗆𝗈𝗇𝗈𝗂𝖽 ​ U { M } \displaystyle\vDash{\mathsf{submonoid}\,U_{\{M\}}} 
 (4) 
 (1) is 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟣 \mathsf{Thm1} ; (2) follows immediately from (1); (3)
follows from (1) by part 12 of Lemma  B.1 ; and
(4) follows from (1), (2), (3), and 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟣 \mathsf{Def1} . . Therefore,
 ( ⋆ ) (\star) holds.
 □ \Box 
 8. 
 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟤 \mathsf{Def2} : ⋅ ( M × M ) → M op = λ p : M × M . ( 𝗌𝗇𝖽 p ) ⋅ ( 𝖿𝗌𝗍 p ) {{\cdot^{\rm op}_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}}}={\lambda\,p:{M}\times{M}\mathrel{.}{(\mathsf{snd}\,p)}\mathrel{\cdot}{(\mathsf{fst}\,p)}}} (opposite of ⋅ \cdot ).

 9. 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟩 \mathsf{Thm7} : ∀ x , y , z : M . x ⋅ op ( y ⋅ op z ) = ( x ⋅ op y ) ⋅ op z {\forall\,x,y,z:M\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot^{\rm op}}{({y}\mathrel{\cdot^{\rm op}}{z})}}={{({x}\mathrel{\cdot^{\rm op}}{y})}\mathrel{\cdot^{\rm op}}{z}}} 
 x ( ⋅ op \cdot^{\rm op} is associative).
 Proof of the theorem. 
Let A o \textbf{A}_{o} be
 x ⋅ op ( y ⋅ op z ) = ( x ⋅ op y ) ⋅ op z {{x}\mathrel{\cdot^{\rm op}}{({y}\mathrel{\cdot^{\rm op}}{z})}}={{({x}\mathrel{\cdot^{\rm op}}{y})}\mathrel{\cdot^{\rm op}}{z}} 
 and T = ( L , Γ ) T=(L,\Gamma) be 𝖬𝖮𝖭 \mathsf{MON} extended by Def2 . We
must show
 ( ⋆ ) ​ T ⊨ ∀ x , y , z : M . A o . (\star){\ }T\vDash\forall\,x,y,z:M\mathrel{.}\textbf{A}_{o}. 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ ( x : M ) ↓ ∧ ( y : M ) ↓ ∧ ( z : M ) ↓ \displaystyle\vDash({x}:{M}){\downarrow}\land({y}:{M}){\downarrow}\land({z}:{M}){\downarrow} 
 (1) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ ( z ⋅ y ) ⋅ x = z ⋅ ( y ⋅ x ) \displaystyle\vDash{{{({z}\mathrel{\cdot}{y})}\mathrel{\cdot}{x}}={{z}\mathrel{\cdot}{({y}\mathrel{\cdot}{x})}}} 
 (2) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ A o \displaystyle\vDash\textbf{A}_{o} 
 (3) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ ∀ x , y , z : M . A o \displaystyle\vDash{\forall\,x,y,z:M\mathrel{.}\textbf{A}_{o}} 
 (4) 
 (1) follows from variables always being defined by [ 20 , Axiom A5.1] ;
(2) follows from (1) and Axiom 1 of T T by Universal Instantiation
 [ 20 , Theorem A.14] and the Equality Rules [ 20 , Theorem A.13] ;
(3) follows from Lemma  B.4 and (2)
by repeated applications of Rule R2 ′ \mbox{R2}^{\prime} [ 20 , Lemma A.2] 
using ( ⋆ ⋆ ) ({\star}{\star}) the fact that ( x : M ) ({x}:{M}) , ( y : M ) ({y}:{M}) , and
 ( z : M ) ({z}:{M}) are not free in Γ \Gamma since Γ \Gamma is a set of sentences; and
(4) follows from (3) by Universal Generalization [ 20 , Theorem A.30] 
again using ( ⋆ ⋆ ) ({\star}{\star}) .
Therefore ( ⋆ ) (\star) holds.
 □ \Box 
 10. 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟪 \mathsf{Thm8} : ∀ x : M . 𝖾 ⋅ op x = x ⋅ op 𝖾 = x {\forall\,x:M\mathrel{.}{{\mathsf{e}}\mathrel{\cdot^{\rm op}}{x}}\mathrel{=}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot^{\rm op}}{\mathsf{e}}}\mathrel{=}{x}}

 Proof of the theorem.

 𝖾 ⋅ op x = x ⋅ op 𝖾 = x {{{\mathsf{e}}\mathrel{\cdot^{\rm op}}{x}}\mathrel{=}{{x}\mathrel{\cdot^{\rm op}}{\mathsf{e}}}\mathrel{=}{x}} 
 and T = ( L , Γ ) T=(L,\Gamma) be 𝖬𝖮𝖭 \mathsf{MON} extended by Def2 . We

 (1) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ x ⋅ 𝖾 = 𝖾 ⋅ x = x \displaystyle\vDash{{{x}\mathrel{\cdot}{\mathsf{e}}}\mathrel{=}{{\mathsf{e}}\mathrel{\cdot}{x}}\mathrel{=}{x}} 
 (2)

 ⊨ A o \displaystyle\vDash\textbf{A}_{o}

 (1) follows from variables always being defined by [ 20 , Axiom A5.1] ;

 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟥 \mathsf{Def3} : ⊙ ( { M } × { M } ) → { M } = set-op ( ( M × M ) → M ) → ( ( { M } × { M } ) → { M } ) ⋅ {{\odot_{{({\{M\}}\times{\{M\}})}\rightarrow{\{M\}}}}={{\textsf{set-op}}_{{({({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M})}\rightarrow{({({\{M\}}\times{\{M\}})}\rightarrow{\{M\}})}}\,\cdot}}

 12. 
 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟦 \mathsf{Def4} : 𝖤 { M } = { 𝖾 M } {{\mathsf{E}_{\{M\}}}={\{\mathsf{e}_{M}\}}} (set identity element).
 Proof that RHS is defined. 
We must show that MON ⊨ { 𝖾 M } ↓ \textsf{MON}\vDash\{\mathsf{e}_{M}\}{\downarrow} . Now { 𝖾 M } \{\mathsf{e}_{M}\} stands for
 ( λ x 1 : M . λ x : M . x = x 1 ) ( 𝖾 M ) . (\lambda\,x_{1}:M\mathrel{.}\lambda\,x:M\mathrel{.}x=x_{1})\,(\mathsf{e}_{M}). 
 Since constants are always defined by  [ 20 , Axiom A5.2] ,
 { 𝖾 M } \{\mathsf{e}_{M}\} beta-reduces [ 20 , Theorem 7.1 and Axiom
A4] to
 λ ​ x : M . x = e M . \lambda\,x:M\mathrel{.}x=\textsf{e}_{M}. 
 Since every function abstraction is defined by  [ 20 , Axiom
A5.11] , we have { 𝖾 M } ↓ \{\mathsf{e}_{M}\}{\downarrow} by
Quasi-Equality Substitution [ 20 , Lemma A.2] .
 □ \Box 
 13. 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟫 \mathsf{Thm9} : ∀ x , y , z : { M } . x ⊙ ( y ⊙ z ) = ( x ⊙ y ) ⊙ z {\forall\,x,y,z:\{M\}\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\odot}{({y}\mathrel{\odot}{z})}}={{({x}\mathrel{\odot}{y})}\mathrel{\odot}{z}}} 
 ( ⊙ \odot is associative).
 Proof of the theorem. 
Let T = ( L , Γ ) T=(L,\Gamma) be 𝖬𝖮𝖭 \mathsf{MON} extended by 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟥 \mathsf{Def3} .
We must show
 ( ⋆ ) ​ T ⊨ ∀ x , y , z : { M } . x ⊙ ( y ⊙ z ) = ( x ⊙ y ) ⊙ z . (\star){\ }T\vDash\forall\,x,y,z:\{M\}\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\odot}{({y}\mathrel{\odot}{z})}}={{({x}\mathrel{\odot}{y})}\mathrel{\odot}{z}}. 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ ( x : { M } ) ↓ ∧ ( y : { M } ) ↓ ∧ ( z : { M } ) ↓ \displaystyle\vDash{({x}:{\{M\}}){\downarrow}}\land{({y}:{\{M\}}){\downarrow}}\land{({z}:{\{M\}}){\downarrow}} 
 (1) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ x ⊙ ( y ⊙ z ) = \displaystyle\vDash{{{x}\mathrel{\odot}{({y}\mathrel{\odot}{z})}}={}} 
 { d : M ∣ ∃ a : x , b : y , c : z . d = a ⋅ ( b ⋅ c ) } \displaystyle\phantom{{}\vDash{}}\hskip 8.61108pt{\{{{d}:{M}}\mid{\exists\,a:x,\,b:y,\,c:z\mathrel{.}{d}={{a}\mathrel{\cdot}{({b}\mathrel{\cdot}{c})}}}\}} 
 (2) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ ( x ⊙ y ) ⊙ z = \displaystyle\vDash{{{({x}\mathrel{\odot}{y})}\mathrel{\odot}{z}}={}} 
 { d : M ∣ ∃ a : x , b : y , c : z . d = ( a ⋅ b ) ⋅ c } \displaystyle\phantom{{}\vDash{}}\hskip 8.61108pt{\{{{d}:{M}}\mid{\exists\,a:x,\,b:y,\,c:z\mathrel{.}{d}={{({a}\mathrel{\cdot}{b})}\mathrel{\cdot}{c}}}\}} 
 (3) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ x ⊙ ( y ⊙ z ) = ( x ⊙ y ) ⊙ z \displaystyle\vDash{{{x}\mathrel{\odot}{({y}\mathrel{\odot}{z})}}={{({x}\mathrel{\odot}{y})}\mathrel{\odot}{z}}} 
 (4) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ ∀ x , y , z : { M } . x ⊙ ( y ⊙ z ) = ( x ⊙ y ) ⊙ z \displaystyle\vDash{\forall\,x,y,z:\{M\}\mathrel{.}{{x}\mathrel{\odot}{({y}\mathrel{\odot}{z})}}={{({x}\mathrel{\odot}{y})}\mathrel{\odot}{z}}} 
 (5) 
 (1) follows from variables always being defined by  [ 20 , Axiom A5.1] ;
(2) and (3) follow from (1) and 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟥 \mathsf{Def3} ;
(4) follows from (2) and (3) by Axiom 1 of  T T ; and
(5) follows from (4) by Universal Generalization [ 20 , Theorem A.30] 
using the fact that x x , y y , and z z are not free in Γ \Gamma since
 Γ \Gamma is a set of sentences. Therefore, ( ⋆ ) (\star) holds.
 □ \Box 
 14. 
 𝖳𝗁𝗆𝟣𝟢 \mathsf{Thm10} : ∀ x : { M } . 𝖤 ⊙ x = x ⊙ 𝖤 = x {\forall\,x:\{M\}\mathrel{.}{{\mathsf{E}}\mathrel{\odot}{x}}\mathrel{=}{{x}\mathrel{\odot}{\mathsf{E}}}\mathrel{=}{x}} 
 x 
 ( 𝖤 \mathsf{E} is an identity element with
respect to ⊙ \odot ).
 Proof of the theorem. 
Let T = ( L , Γ ) T=(L,\Gamma) be MON extended by 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟥 \mathsf{Def3} and
 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟦 \mathsf{Def4} . We must show
 ( ⋆ ) ​ T ⊨ ∀ x : { M } . 𝖤 ⊙ x = x ⊙ 𝖤 = x . (\star){\ }T\vDash{\forall\,x:\{M\}\mathrel{.}{{\mathsf{E}}\mathrel{\odot}{x}}\mathrel{=}{{x}\mathrel{\odot}{\mathsf{E}}}\mathrel{=}{x}}. 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ ( x : { M } ) ↓ \displaystyle\vDash{({x}:{\{M\}}){\downarrow}} 
 (1) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ 𝖤 ↓ \displaystyle\vDash{\mathsf{E}{\downarrow}} 
 (2) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ 𝖤 ⊙ x = { b : M ∣ ∃ a : x . b = 𝖾 ⋅ a } \displaystyle\vDash{{{\mathsf{E}}\mathrel{\odot}{x}}={\{{{b}:{M}}\mid{\exists\,a:x\mathrel{.}{b}={{\mathsf{e}}\mathrel{\cdot}{a}}}\}}} 
 (3) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ x ⊙ 𝖤 = { b : M ∣ ∃ a : x . b = a ⋅ 𝖾 } \displaystyle\vDash{{{x}\mathrel{\odot}{\mathsf{E}}}={\{{{b}:{M}}\mid{\exists\,a:x\mathrel{.}{b}={{a}\mathrel{\cdot}{\mathsf{e}}}}\}}} 
 (4) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ 𝖤 ⊙ x = x ⊙ 𝖤 = x \displaystyle\vDash{{{\mathsf{E}}\mathrel{\odot}{x}}\mathrel{=}{{x}\mathrel{\odot}{\mathsf{E}}}\mathrel{=}{x}} 
 (5) 
 Γ \displaystyle\Gamma 
 ⊨ ∀ x : { M } . 𝖤 ⊙ x = x ⊙ 𝖤 = x \displaystyle\vDash{\forall\,x:\{M\}\mathrel{.}{{\mathsf{E}}\mathrel{\odot}{x}}\mathrel{=}{{x}\mathrel{\odot}{\mathsf{E}}}\mathrel{=}{x}} 
 (6) 
 (1) follows from variables always being defined by  [ 20 , Axiom A5.1] ;
(2) follows from constants always defined by  [ 20 , Axiom A5.2] ;
(3) and (4) follow from (1), (2), 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟥 \mathsf{Def3} , and 𝖣𝖾𝖿𝟦 \mathsf{Def4} ;
(5) follows from (3) and (4) by Axiom 2 of  T T ; and
(6) follows from (5) by Universal Generalization [ 20 , Theorem A.30] 
using the fact that x x is not free in Γ \Gamma since
 Γ \Gamma is a set of sentences. Therefore, ( ⋆ ) (\star) holds.
 □ \Box 
 15. 
 Thm11 (Thm1-via-MON-to-opposite-monoid) :
   𝖬𝖮𝖭𝖮𝖨𝖣 ( U { M } , ⋅ ( M × M ) → M op , 𝖾 M ) {\mathsf{MONOID}(U_{\{M\}},\cdot^{\rm op}_{{({M}\times{M})}\rightarrow{M}},\mathsf{e}_{M})} 
 (opposite monoids are monoids).
 Proof of the theorem. 
Let T T be the top theory of MON-1 . We must show T ⊨ Thm11 T\vDash\textsf{Thm11} . We have previously proved ( ⋆ ) (\star) MON ⊨ Thm1 \textsf{MON}\vDash\textsf{Thm1} . Φ = MON-to-opposite-monoid \Phi=\textsf{MON-to-opposite-monoid} is a
development morphism from MON to MON-1 , and so
 Φ ~ = ( μ , ν ) \widetilde{\Phi}=(\mu,\nu) is a theory morphism from MON 
to T T . Thus ( ⋆ ) (\star) implies T ⊨ ν ⁡ ( Thm1 ) T\vDash\nu(\textsf{Thm1}) .
Therefore, T ⊨ Thm11 T\vDash\textsf{Thm11} since Thm11 = ν ⁡ ( Thm1 ) \textsf{Thm11}=\nu(\textsf{Thm1}) .
 □ \Box
